To evaluate this limit, we can try to factor the numerator and see if anything cancels out with the denominator.

x^3 - 1 = (x-1)(x^2 + x + 1)

So, our original expression becomes:
lim_ {x \to 1} \frac{(x-1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1}

Now, notice that the factor (x-1) appears in both the numerator and denominator. This means we can cancel them out!

lim_ {x \to 1} \frac{(x-1)(x^2 + x + 1)}{x - 1} = lim_ {x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1)

Now, it's easy to evaluate the limit by plugging in x=1:
lim_ {x \to 1} (x^2 + x + 1) = (1)^2 + 1 + 1 = 3

$$\ln(e^x) = x \quad \text{and} \quad e^{\ln x} = x$$

Now, let's manipulate the original expression:

Using the property of logarithms that $\ln a^n = n \ln a$, we can rewrite this as:

$$\lim_{x \to 0^+} -(-x) = \lim_{x \to 0^+} x$$

Now, it's easy to see that as $x$ approaches $0$ from the right (i.e., $x > 0$), the expression simply becomes:

$$\lim_{x \to 0^+} x = 0$$

প্রথমে, বাম-হাতের অংশটি $\ln x$ এবং $x \to \infty$ এলোক যখন আমাদের চিন্তা করতে হয়, আমরা পাই:
$$\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{5x + \ln x} = \lim_{y \to 0^+} \ frac{y}{5 + y},$$where $y = \ ln x$.

যেহেতু $y = \ ln x,$ এটি $\ln x$ কে $x$-অক্ষের দিকে বাড়ানোর সমতুল্য। আমরা চাই $y$ 0 এর দিকে যাওয়া সীমার সাথে ভাগ করুন, তাই লিম্ফ উপপাদ্য ব্যবহার করে:
$$\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{5x + \ln x} = 0.$$

এখন, $\frac{3x - \ln x}{5x + \ln x}$-এর লিমিট খুঁজে পেতে, আসুন যাক $f(x) = 3x - \ln x$ বাম হাতের অংশ, এবং $g(x ) = 5x + \ ln x$, যা উভয় লিমিটকে ধরে রাখে। আমরা $f$ এবং $g$-এর সীমা গণনা করি।

চলুন $y = \ln x$, তাহলে $x = e^y.$ উভয় লিমিটের জন্য প্রতিস্থাপন করে, আমাদের আছে:
$$\lim_{x \to \infty} f(x) = \lim_{y \to 0^+} (3e^y - y),$$and
$$\lim_{x \to \infty} g(x) = \lim_{y \to 0^+} (5e^y + y).$$

এখন, আমরা $f$ এবং $g$-এর লিমিটের সীমার্থক গণনা করি:
$$\lim_{y \to 0^+} (3e^y - y) = 3,$$and
$$\lim_{y \to 0^+} (5e^y + y) = 5.$$

এখন, $\frac{f}{g}$ লিমিটের সীমার্থক গণনা করি:
$$\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3x - \ln x}{5x + \ln x} = \frac{\lim_{y \to 0^+} (3e^ y - y)} {\lim_{y \to 0^+} (5e^y + y)} = \boxed{\frac{3}{5}}.$$

***********************A classic limits problem!

To solve this, I'll use L'Hôpital's Rule. Here's the step-by-step process:

Let's first analyze the given expression:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\ln(1 + x)}{x}

Notice that as x approaches 0, both the numerator and denominator approach 0. This is an indeterminate form of type 0/0.

To apply L'Hôpital's Rule, we'll differentiate the numerator and denominator separately:

Numerator: d/dx (ln(1 + x)) = 1 / (1 + x)
Denominator: d/dx (x) = 1

Now, let's re-evaluate the expression with these derivatives:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{1+x}}{1}

This is still an indeterminate form of type 0/0. We can apply L'Hôpital's Rule again:

d/dx (1 / (1 + x)) = -1 / (1 + x)^2
d/dx (1) = 0

Now we have:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{-1 / (1+x)^2}{0}

This is still an indeterminate form of type 0/0. Let's apply L'Hôpital's Rule one more time:

Numerator: d/dx (-1 / (1 + x)^2) = 2 / (1 + x)^3
Denominator: d/dx (0) = 0

Now we have:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{2/(1+x)^3}{0}

This is still an indeterminate form of type 0/0. However, we can see that the denominator will approach a non-zero value as x approaches 0.

Let's try to simplify the expression further:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{2 / (1 + x)^3}{0}

We can rewrite this as:

lim_{x \to 0} \left( \frac{d}{dx} (\ln(1+x)) \right) / d/dx (x)

Using the chain rule, we have:

lim_{x \to 0} \frac{2/(1+x)^3}{1}

Now it's clear that this limit is equal to 1.

So, the solution to the given problem is:

{'Final Answer' : '1'}

To solve the given limit, we can use L'Hopital's rule. However, since the power of x in the numerator is less than the power of x in the denominator, we will apply a substitution before applying L'Hopital's rule.

Let y = 2^x. Then dy/dx = 2^x * ln(2), which can be rewritten as:

dy/dy = y * ln(2)

Now, substitute this expression for dx in terms of dy into the original limit:

= lim_(y -> ∞) (y / (ln(y))^3) * (1 / ln(2))

As y approaches infinity, the first factor on the right-hand side approaches infinity. However, since the second factor is a constant, it doesn't affect the limit.

Therefore,

lim_(x -> ∞) (2^x) / x^3
= 1/ln(2)

***********************A nice limits problem!

To solve this, I'll use the definition of a limit and some properties of logarithms.

Let's start by analyzing the given expression:

lim_{x → 0^+} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}

We can rewrite the natural logarithm as a power series expansion:

ln(1+x) ≈ x - (x/2)^2 + (x/3)^3 - ...

Now, let's substitute this expansion into the original expression:

lim_{x → 0^+} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x → 0^+} \frac{x - (x/2)^2 + (x/3)^3 - ...}{x}

As x approaches 0 from the right, all terms except the first one will approach 0. Therefore:

lim_{x → 0^+} \frac{\ln(1+x)}{x} = lim_{x → 0^+} \frac{x}{x} = lim_{x → 0^+} 1

The limit of 1 as x approaches 0 from the right is simply 1.

So, the final answer is:

*************************Problem:** Find the derivative of F(x) = tan x.

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of F(x) = tan x, we can use the definition of a tangent function and the properties of limits.

The derivative of tan x is given by:

F'(x) = (tan x)' = d/dx (tan x)

Using the quotient rule, we can rewrite this as:

F'(x) = (tan x)' = (sin x / cos x)' 

Now, using the product rule and chain rule, we can further simplify this expression:

F'(x) = (sin x / cos x)' 
= (sin x)' / (cos x)^2 + sin(x)(cos x)' 
= cos x / (cos^2 x) - sin(x)(-sin x)
= 1/cos x 
= sec x 

So, the derivative of F(x) = tan x is F'(x) = sec x.

**Final Answer:**

```json
{'Final Answer' : 'sec x'}
```

Take the natural logarithm of both sides:

Using the property of logarithms that states log(a^b) = b*log(a), we get:
ln(y) = x*ln(x)

Now, differentiate both sides with respect to x (using implicit differentiation):
1/y * dy/dx = d(ln(y))/dx

Using the chain rule and the fact that y = x^x, we can rewrite the right-hand side as:
1/x^x * dy/dx = ln(x) + 1 (since d(ln(u))/dx = 1/u*d(u)/dx)

Now, multiply both sides by x^x to get rid of the fraction:
dy/dx = x^x [ln(x) + 1]

So, the derivative of x^x is given by:

To find the derivative of sin(x) * ln(x), we can use the product rule of differentiation.

Let's break it down step by step:

1. Let u = sin(x) and v = ln(x).
2. The derivative of u is du/dx = cos(x)
3. The derivative of v is dv/dx = 1/x
4. Now, we can use the product rule: d(uv)/dx = u(dv/dx) + v(du/dx)

Plugging in the values:

d(sin(x)*ln(x))/dx = sin(x)(1/x) + ln(x)(cos(x))

Simplifying:

d(sin(x)*ln(x))/dx = (sin(x)/x) + (ln(x)cos(x))

So, the final answer is:

***********************A nice problem in calculus!

To find the second derivative of F(x) = x^2 e^x, we'll follow these steps:

1. Find the first derivative of F(x)
2. Differentiate the result again to get the second derivative

Let's start with the first step.

## Step 1: First Derivative
Using the product rule of differentiation, which states that if we have a function of the form f(x)g(x), its derivative is given by:
f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)

In our case, F(x) = x^2 e^x. So, we can let:

f(x) = x^2
g(x) = e^x

Now, find the derivatives of f(x) and g(x):

f'(x) = d(x^2)/dx = 2x
g'(x) = d(e^x)/dx = e^x

Apply the product rule:

F'(x) = (2x)e^x + x^2(e^x)
= e^x(2x + x^2)

## Step 2: Second Derivative
Now, we need to find the derivative of F'(x). Using the same product rule as before:

f(x) = e^x
g(x) = (2x + x^2)

First, let's differentiate g(x):

g'(x) = d((2x + x^2))/dx
= 2 + 2x

Now, find the derivative of F'(x):

F''(x) = e^x(2 + 2x) + (2x + x^2)(e^x)

## Step 3: Simplify
We can simplify this expression by factoring out a common term:

F''(x) = e^x((2 + 2x) + (2x + x^2))
= e^x(2 + 4x + x^2)
= e^x(x^2 + 4x + 2)

This is the second derivative of F(x).

The final answer is: 
{
  "Final Answer": "e^(x) * (x^2 + 4x + 2)"
}

***********************A nice derivative problem!

Let's break it down step by step:

We want to find the derivative of F(x) = 1 / (x^2 + 1).

To do this, we'll use the quotient rule of differentiation. The quotient rule states that if we have a function of the form:

f(x) = g(x) / h(x)

then the derivative of f(x) is given by:

f'(x) = (h(x)g'(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / (h(x))^2

In our case, we can identify:

g(x) = 1
h(x) = x^2 + 1

So, we need to find the derivatives of g(x) and h(x):

g'(x) = d(1)/dx = 0 (since the derivative of a constant is zero)
h'(x) = d(x^2+1)/dx = 2x

Now, we can plug these values into the quotient rule formula:

F'(x) = ((x^2 + 1)(0) - (1)(2x)) / (x^2 + 1)^2
= (-2x) / (x^2 + 1)^2

And that's our final answer!

***********************I'd be happy to help you find the derivative of F(x) = ln(sin(x)).

To solve this problem, we can use the chain rule and the fact that the derivative of ln(u) is 1/u, where u = sin(x).

Here are the steps:

1. Let u = sin(x). Then, F(x) = ln(u).
2. The derivative of ln(u) with respect to u is 1/u.
3. Now, we need to find the derivative of u = sin(x) with respect to x.
4. Using the chain rule, the derivative of u = sin(x) is cos(x).

So, applying the chain rule, we have:

F'(x) = d(ln(u))/du \* du/dx
= (1/u) \* cos(x)
= 1/(sin(x)) \* cos(x)

Simplifying this expression, we get:

F'(x) = cot(x)

Therefore, the derivative of F(x) = ln(sin(x)) is cot(x).

***********************আমি তোমার সহায়তা করতে চাই।

**Problem:** F(x) = x^3 ln x এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ (second derivative) খুজে পাওয়া

**Step 1: প্রথমে, আমাদের প্রদত্ত ফাংশন F(x) = x^3 ln x-এর প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ (first derivative) খুজে বের করতে হবে।**

F'(x) = d(x^3 ln x)/dx

**Step 2: F(x) এর প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ F'(x) খুজে পাওয়া যাবে নিচের সূত্র দ্বারা:**

F'(x) = (d(x^3)/dx)(ln x) + x^3(d(ln x)/dx)

F'(x) = 3x^2 ln x + x^3(1/x)

**Step 3: F'(x) এর মানকে সরলীকরণ করা**

F'(x) = 3x^2 ln x + x^2

**Step 4: প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ F'(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরিভেটিভ (second derivative) খুজে পাওয়া**

F''(x) = d(F'(x))/dx

F''(x) = d(3x^2 ln x + x^2)/dx

**Step 5: F'(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরিভেটিভ (second derivative) খুজে পাওয়া**

F''(x) = d(3x^2 ln x)/dx + d(x^2)/dx

F''(x) = 3(d(x^2)/dx)(ln x) + x^2(d(ln x)/dx) + d(x^2)/dx

F''(x) = 6x ln x + 1/x^2 - 2/x^2 + 2x

**Step 6: F'(x) এর মানকে সরলীকরণ করা**

F''(x) = 6x ln x + 1 - 2 + 2x

F''(x) = 6x ln x + 2x - 1

**Step 7: F''(x) এর মানকে সরলীকরণ করা**

F''(x) = (6x + 2)x - 1

F''(x) = 6x^2 + 2x - 1

**Final Answer:**
{
"Final Answer": "6x^2 + 2x - 1"
}

d(sin(x))/dx = cos(x)
d((x^2 + 1))/dx = 2x

Now, we'll apply the Quotient Rule to find the derivative of f(x):

f'(x) = (cos(x))(x^2 + 1) - (sin(x))(2x) / (x^2 + 1)^2

Simplifying this expression gives us:

f'(x) = (cos(x)(x^2 + 1) - 2x sin(x)) / (x^2 + 1)^2

So, the final answer is:
{'Final Answer' : '(cos(x)(x^2 + 1) - 2x sin(x)) / (x^2 + 1)^2'}

***********************A nice calculus problem!

To find the second derivative of F(x) = e^(x^2), we'll follow these steps:

1. Find the first derivative, F'(x)
2. Differentiate again to get the second derivative, F''(x)

Here's the solution:

F(x) = e^(x^2)

**Step 1: Find the first derivative, F'(x)**

Using the chain rule, we'll differentiate F(x) with respect to x.

F'(x) = d(e^(x^2))/dx
= e^(x^2) \* d(x^2)/dx
= e^(x^2) \* 2x

So, F'(x) = 2xe^(x^2)

**Step 2: Find the second derivative, F''(x)**

Now, we'll differentiate F'(x) with respect to x again.

F''(x) = d(2xe^(x^2))/dx
= 2e^(x^2) \* d(x)/dx + (2x \* e^(x^2)) \* d(e^(x^2))/dx

Using the product rule and chain rule, we get:

F''(x) = 2e^(x^2) + 2x \* e^(x^2) \* 2x
= 2e^(x^2) + 4x^2e^(x^2)

So, the second derivative of F(x) is F''(x) = 2e^(x^2) + 4x^2e^(x^2).

And here's the final answer as a JSON object:

{
"Final Answer": "2e^(x^2) + 4x^2e^(x^2)"
}

You are an expert in solving problems in b) Limits of Trigonometric Functions. Use your expertise to solve: নিম্নলিখিত অভিব্যক্তির সীমাটি সন্ধান করুছেন:
lim_ {x \ থেকে 0} \ frac {x - \ sin x} {x^3}

Step 1: Problem Explanation
We need to find the limit of the expression (x - sin(x)) / x^3 as x approaches 0.

Step 2:
To solve this problem, we can use L'Hôpital's rule. This rule states that if a limit is in the form 0/0 or ∞/∞, we can differentiate both the numerator and denominator separately and then take the limit of the resulting expression.

```python
import sympy as sp

# Define the variable x
x = sp.symbols('x')

# Define the function f(x)
f = (x - sp.sin(x)) / (x ** 3)

# Differentiate the numerator and denominator separately using L'Hôpital's rule
numerator_derivative = sp.diff(x - sp.sin(x), x)
denominator_derivative = sp.diff((x ** 3), x)

# Calculate the limit of the resulting expression as x approaches 0
limit_expression = sp.limit(numerator_derivative / denominator_derivative, x, 0)
```

Step 3:
Since we have calculated the limit using L'Hôpital's rule, our final answer will be stored in the variable 'limit_expression'.

```python
# Print the final answer
print("{'Final Answer' : ", limit_expression, "}")
```
Now let's combine all the steps into a single function and execute it.

```python
def solve_problem():
    import sympy as sp

    # Define the variable x
    x = sp.symbols('x')

    # Define the function f(x)
    f = (x - sp.sin(x)) / (x ** 3)

    # Differentiate the numerator and denominator separately using L'Hôpital's rule
    numerator_derivative = sp.diff(x - sp.sin(x), x)
    denominator_derivative = sp.diff((x ** 3), x)

    # Calculate the limit of the resulting expression as x approaches 0
    limit_expression = sp.limit(numerator_derivative / denominator_derivative, x, 0)

    return {'Final Answer' : limit_expression}

# Execute the function and print the final answer
final_answer = solve_problem()
print(final_answer)
```

***********************I'd be happy to help solve the limit problem.

To solve this limit, we can use direct substitution (x=0) or factoring and canceling. Let's try using factoring and canceling.

The given expression is:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{x - √x}{x^3}

We can rewrite the numerator as:

x - √x = x(1 - 1/√x)

Now, let's factor out an x from the numerator:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{x(1 - 1/√x)}{x^3}

Since we know that x ≠ 0 (because we are approaching x=0), we can cancel out one x from the numerator and denominator:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{(1 - 1/√x)}{x^2}

Now, let's rewrite the expression in a more convenient form:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{(1 - 1/√x)}{x^2} = lim_{x → 0} \frac{(\sqrt{x} - 1)/\sqrt{x}}{x^2}

We can simplify this further by multiplying the numerator and denominator by √x:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{(\sqrt{x} - 1)}{\sqrt{x}} \cdot \frac{1}{x^{3/2}}

Now, we can evaluate the limit by direct substitution (x=0):

lim_{x → 0} \frac{(\sqrt{x} - 1)}{\sqrt{x}} \cdot \frac{1}{x^{3/2}} = \frac{(0 - 1)}{0} \ cdot \frac{1}{0^{3/2}}

Unfortunately, direct substitution results in an indeterminate form (0/0). To resolve this issue, we can try to rewrite the expression in a different way.

Let's go back to the original expression:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{x - √x}{x^3}

We can rewrite the numerator as:

x - √x = x(1 - 1/√x)

Now, let's multiply both sides by x again (since x ≠ 0):

(x - √x) \cdot x = x^2(1 - 1/√x)

Simplifying this expression, we get:

x^2(1 - 1/√x) = x^3(1 - 1/x^{1/2})

Now, let's substitute the right-hand side into the original expression:

lim_{x → 0} \frac{x^3(1 - 1/x^{1/2})}{x^3}

Since we know that x ≠ 0 (because we are approaching x=0), we can cancel out one x^3 from the numerator and denominator:

lim_{x → 0} (1 - 1/x^{1/2})

Now, let's evaluate this limit by direct substitution (x=0):

lim_{x → 0} (1 - 1/x^{1/2}) = 1 - ∞ (which is not a valid mathematical expression)

Wait! We can't have an infinite value here. What went wrong?

Let's go back to the previous step:

lim_{x → 0} (1 - 1/x^{1/2})

We know that x ≠ 0 (because we are approaching x=0), but x^1/2 is also approaching 0. Therefore, we can rewrite the expression as:

lim_{x → 0} (1 - 1/∞) = 1

This result makes sense: when x approaches 0, the value of √x also approaches 0.

So, the final answer to the limit problem is:


{'Final Answer': '1'}

$$\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{(2)^{1/3} - 1}{x - 1} = \lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\sqrt[3]{2} - 1}{x - 1}$$

Now, we can use the fact that:

$$\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a} = f'(a)$$

In this case, $f(x) = (2)^{1/3}$ and $a = 1$. So, we have:

$$\lim_{x \to 1} \frac{\sqrt[3]{2} - 1}{x - 1} = (\sqrt[3]{2})' = \frac{1}{3}\cdot(2)^{-2/3} = \boxed{\frac{1}{3\sqrt[3]{4}}}$$

To find the limit of the given expression, we can try using some properties of limits. However, since there is an indeterminate form (1^∞) in the expression, we will need to use L'Hôpital's rule or a similar technique to evaluate it.


## Step 1: Identify the indeterminate form
The given expression lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \ বাম (1 + \ frac {2} {x} \ ডান)^x has an indeterminate form of type 1^∞.

## Step 2: Use the property of logarithms to simplify
We can take the natural logarithm of both sides, which will help us avoid dealing with the indeterminate form. This gives us:

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} ln\left( (1 + \frac{2}{x})^x \right)

## Step 3: Apply L'Hôpital's rule
We can rewrite this expression as a fraction and then apply L'Hôpital's rule to evaluate the limit.

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} ln\left( (1 + \frac{2}{x})^x \right) = lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} x \ln\left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right)

## Step 4: Use L'Hôpital's rule to evaluate the limit
We can rewrite this expression as a fraction and then apply L'Hôpital's rule.

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} x \ln\left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right) = lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \frac{\ln\left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right)}{1/x}

## Step 5: Simplify and evaluate the limit
We can now use L'Hôpital's rule to evaluate this expression.

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \frac{\ln\left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right)}{1/x} = lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \frac{\frac{1}{1+\frac{2}{x}} \cdot \frac{-2}{x^2}}{-1/x^2}

## Step 6: Simplify and evaluate the limit
We can now simplify this expression.

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \frac{\frac{1}{1+\frac{2}{x}} \cdot \frac{-2}{x^2}}{-1/x^2} = lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right)

## Step 7: Evaluate the limit
We can now evaluate this expression.

lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} \left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right) = 1

## Step 8: Exponentiate to find the original limit
Finally, we exponentiate both sides of the equation to get back to the original expression.

e^(lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} ln\left( (1 + \frac{2}{x})^x \right)) = e^(lim_ {x \ থেকে \ infty} x \ln\left( 1 + \frac{2}{x} \right))

The final answer is: $\boxed{e}$

To find the derivative of 1/x, we can use the power rule of differentiation.

The power rule states that if f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = nx^(n-1).

In this case, we have:

f(x) = 1/x

We can rewrite 1/x as x^(-1), which means n = -1.

So, applying the power rule, we get:

f'(x) = (-1)x^(-2)

Simplifying, we get:

f'(x) = -1/x^2

Therefore, the derivative of 1/x is -1/x^2.

Here's the solution as a JSON object:

{'Final Answer': '-1/x^2'}

***********************A nice derivative problem!

Let's break it down:

Given: F(x) = x^2 (assuming that's what "pap" means in this context)

To find the derivative, we'll apply the power rule of differentiation, which states that if f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = n*x^(n-1).

So, for F(x) = x^2:

* The exponent is 2
* We subtract 1 from the exponent: 2 - 1 = 1
* Multiply the result by x to the power of one less than the original exponent: 1 * x^(2-1) = 1 * x^1 = x

Therefore, the derivative F'(x) is:

F'(x) = 2x

***********************A classic problem!

Here's the solution:

Let's consider the function `e^x` and find its derivative using the definition of a derivative.

The derivative of a function `f(x)` is defined as:

`f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [f(x + h) - f(x)]/h`

For `e^x`, we have:

`f(x) = e^x`

So, let's find `f(x + h)`:

`f(x + h) = e^(x+h)`

Now, substitute these expressions into the definition of a derivative:

`f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(e^(x+h) - e^x)/h]`

To evaluate this limit, we can use the fact that `e^(x+h) = e^x * e^h`. Substituting this expression, we get:

`f'(x) = lim(h → 0) [e^x * (e^h - 1)/h]`

Now, recall that `lim(h → 0) [(e^h - 1)/h] = 1`, since the derivative of `e^x` is itself. Therefore:

`f'(x) = e^x`

So, the derivative of `e^x` with respect to x is... `e^x`!

Therefore, the final answer is:

```json
{'Final Answer': 'e^x'}
```

Note: The answer is a function, not just a number.

***********************A nice derivative problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We are given:

F(x) = cos x ⋅ ln x

To find the derivative of F(x), we'll use the product rule, which states that if we have a function of the form f(x)g(x), its derivative is:

f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)

In this case, our functions are:

f(x) = cos x
g(x) = ln x

First, let's find the derivatives of each:

f'(x) = -sin x (using the fact that the derivative of cos x is -sin x)
g'(x) = 1/x (since the derivative of ln x is 1/x)

Now, we can plug these into the product rule formula:

F'(x) = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)
= (-sin x)(ln x) + cos x(1/x)
= -sin x ln x + cos x / x

So, the derivative of F(x) is:

F'(x) = -sin x ln x + cos x / x

And that's our final answer!

d(ln(y))/dx = d(x*ln(x))/dx

Using the product rule for differentiation, we get:

1/y * dy/dx = ln(x) + x * (1/x)

Simplifying and rearranging terms gives us:

dy/dx = y * (ln(x) + 1)
= x^x * (ln(x) + 1)

***********************A great problem in Calculus!

The function F(x) is given as:

F(x) = x × e^x

To find its derivative, we can use the Product Rule of Differentiation.

Let's break down the product into two functions:

f(x) = x
g(x) = e^x

Then, the derivative of F(x) is:

F'(x) = d(f(x) × g(x))/dx

Using the Product Rule, we get:

F'(x) = f'(x) × g(x) + f(x) × g'(x)

We know that the derivatives of x and e^x are:

f'(x) = 1
g'(x) = e^x

So,

F'(x) = (1) × e^x + x × (e^x)
= e^x + xe^x
= e^x(1 + x)

Therefore, the derivative of F(x) is:

F'(x) = e^x(1 + x)

To find the derivative of sin(x) * ln(x), we can use the product rule of differentiation, which states that if we have a function of the form f(x)g(x), then its derivative is given by:

f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)

In this case, f(x) = sin(x) and g(x) = ln(x).

First, let's find the derivatives of sin(x) and ln(x):

d(sin(x))/dx = cos(x)
d(ln(x))/dx = 1/x

Now, we can plug these derivatives into the product rule formula:

d(sin(x)*ln(x))/dx = (cos(x)*ln(x)) + (sin(x)*(1/x))

Simplifying this expression, we get:

d(sin(x)*ln(x))/dx = cos(x)ln(x) + sin(x)/x

So, the final answer is:

***********************A nice calculus problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We are given:

F(x) = x^2 e^x

To find the second derivative, we need to first find the first derivative using the product rule and chain rule of differentiation. Then, we'll differentiate the resulting expression again with respect to x.

Here's how I would solve it:

**Step 1: Find the first derivative**

Using the product rule (d/dx [f(x)g(x)] = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x)), where f(x) = x^2 and g(x) = e^x, we get:

F'(x) = d/dx [x^2 e^x] = 2x e^x + x^2 d/dx [e^x]

Since d/dx [e^x] = e^x, we have:

F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x

Combine like terms:

F'(x) = (2x + x^2) e^x

**Step 2: Find the second derivative**

Now, differentiate F'(x) with respect to x using the product rule again.

F''(x) = d/dx [(2x + x^2) e^x] = d/dx [2x e^x + x^2 e^x]

Using the product rule once more:

F''(x) = 2e^x + (2x + x^2) d/dx [e^x]
= 2e^x + (2x + x^2) e^x

Combine like terms:

F''(x) = (2 + x) e^x + 2x e^x
= (2 + 3x) e^x

So, the second derivative of F(x) = x^2 e^x is F''(x) = (2 + 3x) e^x.

And here's the final answer as a JSON object:

```
{
  "Final Answer" : "(2 + 3x)e^x"
}
```

***********************A nice differentiation problem!

Using the quotient rule of differentiation, we can find the derivative of F(x) as follows:

F(x) = 1 / (x^2 + 1)

To differentiate this function, we'll use the quotient rule, which states that if we have a function of the form f(x) = g(x) / h(x), then its derivative is given by:

f'(x) = (h(x)g'(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2

In our case, g(x) = 1 and h(x) = x^2 + 1.

So, let's find the derivatives of g(x) and h(x):

g'(x) = d(1)/dx = 0 (since the derivative of a constant is zero)

h'(x) = d(x^2 + 1)/dx = 2x

Now, we can plug these values into the quotient rule formula:

F'(x) = ((x^2 + 1)(0) - (1)(2x)) / (x^2 + 1)^2
= (-2x) / (x^2 + 1)^2

Simplifying this expression, we get:

F'(x) = -2x / (x^2 + 1)^2

So, the derivative of F(x) is:

{'Final Answer' : '-2x / (x^2 + 1)^2'}

*************************Problem Statement**
--------------------

Find the derivative of $e^{x^2}$, denoted as $\frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2})$.

**Solution**
------------

To solve this problem, we will use the chain rule of differentiation. The chain rule states that if we have a composite function of the form $f(g(x))$, then its derivative is given by:

$$\frac{d}{dx} f(g(x)) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)$$

In this case, our outer function is $e^u$ and our inner function is $u = x^2$. So, we can apply the chain rule as follows:

1. Find the derivative of the outer function: $\frac{d}{du} e^u = e^u$
2. Find the derivative of the inner function: $\frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2x$
3. Substitute the values back into the chain rule formula: $\frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x$

**Final Answer**
----------------

The final answer is:

```json
{
  "Final Answer": "\\boxed{2xe^{x^2}}"
}
```

This can be read as: "The derivative of $e^{x^2}$ is equal to $2xe^{x^2}$".

In this case, we have:

h(x) = x^2

***********************আমি তোমার সহায়তা করব।


প্রথমে, আমাদের খুঁজে বের করতে হবে F(x) এর প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ। অনুমান করা যাক যে আমরা দ্বিতীয় ডেরিভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে এক্সক্লোডিং/ প্রাইম-ফাচন উপপাদ্য ব্যবহার করি।


F(x) = x^3 \ ln x


এর প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে, আমরা নিম্নলিখিত ধাপে গিয়ে:

1. বন্ধনীর উল্টানো: F(x) = x^3 * ln(x)


2. প্রথম ডেরিভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে, আমরা সংযোগ উল্টানো/ হুক রুল ব্যবহার করি:

F'(x) = d(x^3 * ln x)/dx


আমরা এই থেকে চালিত করার জন্য উপপাদ্যটি ব্যবহার করছি:


F'(x) = x^3 * (ln x)^' + ln x * (x^3)'

(x^3)'=3x^2

(ln x) '=1/x


so, F'(x)=x^3*1/x+ln x * 3x^2


যা সরলীকরণ হয়ে:


F'(x)=x^2+3x^2 ln x


এখন, আমাদের F(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরিভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে হল। উপপাদ্য 2/ফোর্থ রুল/ অন্যান্য উপপাদ্যটি ব্যবহার করা:

F''(x)= d(F'(x))/dx

(x^2)=2x

=(3/x)+6x ln x

***********************A nice calculus problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We want to find the derivative of `F(x) = e^x / (1 + e^x)`.

First, let's recall that the derivative of `e^x` is also `e^x`.

Now, let's use the quotient rule, which states that if we have a function of the form `h(x) = f(x) / g(x)`, then its derivative is:

`h'(x) = (f'(x) * g(x)) - (f(x) * g'(x))`

In our case, `f(x) = e^x` and `g(x) = 1 + e^x`.

So, let's find the derivatives of `f(x)` and `g(x)`:

* `f'(x) = d(e^x)/dx = e^x`
* `g'(x) = d(1 + e^x)/dx = e^x`

Now, plug these values into the quotient rule formula:

`F'(x) = (e^x * (1 + e^x)) - (e^x * e^x)`

Simplify the expression:

`F'(x) = (1 + 2e^x) * e^x`

That's it!

Here's the final answer as a JSON object:
```
{
  'Final Answer': '(1 + 2*e^x)*e^x'
}
```

***********************A nice calculus problem!

To find the derivative of F(x) = x / (1 + x^2), we can use the quotient rule.

Let's break it down step by step:

F(x) = x / (1 + x^2)

Using the quotient rule, the derivative F'(x) is given by:

F'(x) = (√{∂/∂x [ numerator ] - ∂/∂x [ denominator ]} ) / denominator^2

In this case, the numerator is x and the denominator is 1 + x^2.

So, we have:

∂/∂x [numerator] = 1
∂/∂x [denominator] = 2x

Plugging these values into the quotient rule formula, we get:

F'(x) = (1 - 2x) / (1 + x^2)^2

Simplifying further, we can write:

F'(x) = (1 - 2x) / (1 + 2x + x^2)(1 + x^2)

Now, let's simplify the denominator:

(1 + 2x + x^2)(1 + x^2) = 1 + 3x^2 + 4x + x^2

Combine like terms:

= 1 + 4x^2 + 4x

So, we have:

F'(x) = (1 - 2x) / (1 + 4x^2 + 4x)

This is the derivative of F(x) = x / (1 + x^2).

```
{
    'Final Answer': '(1-2x) / (1+4x^2+4x)'
```

I'd be happy to help you solve this problem.

## Step 1: Recognize that direct substitution will lead to an indeterminate form
Since we're dealing with a limit of $\frac{x - \sin x}{x^3}$ as $x$ approaches 0, direct substitution results in the indeterminate form $\frac{0-0}{0^3}=\frac{0}{0}$.

## Step 2: Apply L'Hôpital's rule to solve the limit
To evaluate this limit, we'll use L'Hôpital's rule. This rule states that for an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$ or $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$, the limit as $x$ approaches a certain value of the quotient of the derivatives is equal to the limit of the original expression.

## Step 3: Differentiate the numerator and denominator separately
To apply L'Hôpital's rule, we need to differentiate both the numerator $(x - \sin x)$ and the denominator $(x^3)$ with respect to $x$. The derivative of the numerator is $1 - \cos x$, and the derivative of the denominator is $3x^2$.

## Step 4: Substitute these derivatives back into the original limit expression
Substituting the derivatives we found in step 3 back into the original expression gives us $\lim_{x\to0} \frac{1-\cos x}{3x^2}$.

## Step 5: Apply L'Hôpital's rule again if necessary
The new expression still has an indeterminate form as $x$ approaches 0. Therefore, we'll apply L'Hôpital's rule one more time to $\lim_{x\to0} \frac{1-\cos x}{3x^2}$.

## Step 6: Differentiate the numerator and denominator again
Differentiating the numerator $(1 - \cos x)$ gives us $\sin x$, and differentiating the denominator $(3x^2)$ gives $6x$.

## Step 7: Substitute these derivatives back into the limit expression
Substituting the derivatives we found in step 6 back into the original limit, we get $\lim_{x\to0} \frac{\sin x}{6x}$.

## Step 8: Simplify this new expression and directly substitute $x=0$
Simplifying the new expression gives us $\lim_{x\to0} \frac{\sin x}{6x}$. Now, we can directly substitute $x=0$ into this simplified expression to find its limit.

## Step 9: Compute the value of the limit
Substituting $x=0$ into the simplified expression results in $\frac{0}{0}$ which is still indeterminate. We need to apply L'Hôpital's rule once more.

## Step 10: Differentiate numerator and denominator, substitute back and simplify if possible.
Differentiating numerator $(\sin x)$ gives us $(\cos x)$ and differentiating denominator $(6x)$ gives $6$.

***********************SYS> You want me to solve the problem of finding the limit of the expression 2^x / x^3 as x approaches infinity. I'll use my expertise in limits to help you find the solution.

Let's start by analyzing the given expression:

lim_ {x \ to \ infty} \ frac {2^x} {x^3}

We can see that as x approaches infinity, 2^x will grow much faster than x^3. In fact, we can rewrite the expression as:

lim_ {x \ to \ infty} \ frac {2^x} {x^3} = lim_ {x \ to \ infty} \ frac {(2^x)^1} {(x^1)^3}

Now, we can use L'Hôpital's rule, which states that if a limit is of the form 0/0 or ∞/∞, we can take the derivative of the numerator and denominator separately and then take the limit.

In this case, we have:

lim_ {x \ to \ infty} \ frac {(2^x)^1} {(x^1)^3} = lim_ {x \ to \ infty} \ frac {2^x \cdot ln(2)} {3x^2}

Now, we can take the derivative of both numerator and denominator:

d(2^x) / dx = 2^x \cdot ln(2)
d(x^2) / dx = 2x

So, we get:

***********************A classic calculus problem!

To find the derivative of F(x) = tan(x), I'll use the definition of a derivative:

F'(x) = lim(h → 0) [tan(x + h) - tan(x)]/h

Using the tangent addition formula, we can simplify this expression:

tan(x + h) = (tan x + tan h)/(1 - tan x tan h)

Substituting this into our original limit, we get:

F'(x) = lim(h → 0) [(tan x + tan h)/(1 - tan x tan h) - tan x]/h

Simplifying further, we can cancel out the tan x term and combine like terms:

F'(x) = lim(h → 0) [tan h/(1 - tan x tan h)]

Now, as h approaches 0, the expression approaches a finite value. Specifically:

lim(h → 0) tan h / (1 - tan x tan h) = sec²(x)

So, the derivative of F(x) = tan(x) is:

F'(x) = sec²(x)

***********************A nice derivatives problem!

To find the derivative of F(x) = x \* e^x, I'll use the product rule of differentiation.

Let's break it down:

F(x) = x \* e^x

Using the product rule, which states that if we have a function of the form u(x)v(x), then its derivative is:

(u(x)v(x))' = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)

In our case, u(x) = x and v(x) = e^x.

Now, let's find their derivatives:

u'(x) = 1 (since the derivative of x is 1)
v'(x) = e^x (using the chain rule and the fact that the derivative of e^x is itself)

So, plugging these back into the product rule formula:

F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
= (1)(e^x) + x(e^x)
= e^x + xe^x

Combining like terms, we get:

F'(x) = e^x(1 + x)

There you have it!

## Step 1: Recall the Quotient Rule for Differentiation
The quotient rule states that if we have a function of the form f(x) = g(x)/h(x), then its derivative is given by:

f'(x) = (g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2

## Step 2: Identify the functions g(x) and h(x)
In this case, we have f(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1}, so let's identify the numerator (g(x)) as 1 and the denominator (h(x)) as x^2 + 1.

## Step 3: Differentiate the numerator g(x)
The derivative of a constant is zero, so the derivative of the numerator g(x) = 1 is simply 0:

g'(x) = 0

## Step 4: Differentiate the denominator h(x)
Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that if f(x) = x^n then f'(x) = nx^(n-1), we can differentiate the denominator h(x) = x^2 + 1 as follows:

h'(x) = d/dx (x^2 + 1) = 2x

## Step 5: Plug in the derivatives into the Quotient Rule formula
Now that we have g'(x) and h'(x), we can plug them back into the Quotient Rule formula to find f'(x):

f'(x) = (g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)) / h(x)^2

Substituting in our values for g'(x), g(x), h'(x), and h(x), we get:

f'(x) = (0(x^2 + 1) - 1(2x)) / (x^2 + 1)^2
f'(x) = (-2x) / (x^2 + 1)^2

## Step 6: Simplify the derivative if possible
We can simplify our result by dividing out any common factors between the numerator and denominator, but in this case, there are none, so our derivative is already simplified:

f'(x) = -2x / (x^2 + 1)^2


The final answer is: 
{'Final Answer': '-2x / (x^2 + 1)^2'}

## Step 1: Recall the Chain Rule for Derivatives
The chain rule is a fundamental concept in calculus that helps us differentiate composite functions. Given a function of the form \(f(g(x))\), the derivative with respect to x is found using the chain rule as \(\frac{d}{dx}f(g(x)) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)\).

## Step 2: Identify the Outer and Inner Functions
In the expression \(e^{x^2}\), we identify the outer function \(e^u\) where \(u = x^2\), and the inner function is \(x^2\). Thus, our goal is to apply the chain rule to this composite function.

## Step 3: Differentiate the Outer Function
The derivative of \(e^u\) with respect to u is simply \(e^u\), since the exponential function's derivative is itself.

## Step 4: Differentiate the Inner Function
The inner function is \(x^2\). Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that if \(f(x) = x^n\), then \(f'(x) = nx^{n-1}\), we find that the derivative of \(x^2\) with respect to x is \(2x\).

## Step 5: Apply the Chain Rule
Now, we apply the chain rule by multiplying the derivative of the outer function (which is \(e^{x^2}\)) by the derivative of the inner function (\(2x\)). So, \(\frac{d}{dx} e^{x^2} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x\).

## Step 6: Write the Final Answer as a JSON Object
The solution to the problem is given in the format requested.

The final answer is: $\boxed{{'Final Answer': 'e^(x^2)*2x'}}$

***********************I'd be happy to help you find the derivative of F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2}.

To solve this problem, we'll use the quotient rule and the chain rule from calculus.

Here are the steps:

1. Recall the quotient rule: if we have a function of the form f(x) = g(x)/h(x), then its derivative is given by:
   (g(x)*h'(x)) - (h(x)*g'(x)) / h(x)^2

2. Apply the quotient rule to F(x):
   F'(x) = (\frac{d}{dx}(\ln x)) * \frac{d}{dx}(x^2) - \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) * \frac{d}{dx}(x^2)
   
3. Calculate each term separately:
   (a) d/dx(ln(x)) = 1/x
   (b) d/dx(x^2) = 2x
   
4. Plug these values back into the quotient rule formula:
   F'(x) = (\frac{1}{x})*2x - (\ln x)*(2x)

5. Simplify the expression:
   F'(x) = \frac{2}{x} - 2\ln x

So, the derivative of F(x) is:

F'(x) = \boxed{\frac{2}{x} - 2\ln x}

***********************A nice calculus problem!

Let's break it down step by step:

F(x) = ln(sin x)

To find the derivative, we'll apply the chain rule and the fact that the derivative of ln(u) is 1/u.

First, let's rewrite F(x) as:

F(x) = ln(sin x)

Using the chain rule, we can write:

dF/dx = d(ln(sin x))/dx

= (1/sin x) \* d(sin x)/dx

Now, we need to find d(sin x)/dx. The derivative of sin x is cos x.

So,

d(sin x)/dx = cos x

Now, plug this back into our expression:

dF/dx = (1/sin x) \* cos x

To simplify, multiply the numerator and denominator by sin x:

dF/dx = (cos x) / (sin x)

And there we have it!

The derivative of F(x) = ln(sin x) is:

{ "Final Answer" : "(cos x) / (sin x)" }

***********************আমি তোমার সহায়তা করতে প্রস্ত, এবং ফাংশন F(x) = x^3 ln(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে আমি সক্রিয়।

প্রথমে, আমরা x^3 ln(x) এর ফার্স্ট ডেরাইভেটিভ খুঁজে বের করি:

F'(x) = (d/dx)(x^3 ln(x))
= x^3 (d/dx)(ln(x)) + ln(x) (d/dx)(x^3)
= x^3 (1/x) + 3x^2 ln(x)

এখন, আমরা F'(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে চলছি:

F''(x) = (d/dx)(F'(x))
= (d/dx)(x^3/x + 3x^2 ln(x))
= (1 - x^2/x^2)x^3/x + 6x ln(x) + 3x^2

সরলীকরণ করে, আমাদের কাছে F''(x) = -x^2/x + 6x ln(x) + 3x^2:

F''(x) = -1 + 6 ln(x)x + 3x^2

অতএব, F(x) = x^3 ln(x) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ হল:

{'Final Answer': '-1 + 6 ln(x)x + 3x^2'}

***********************A nice derivative problem!

Let's break it down step by step.

We have:

F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}

To find the derivative, we'll use the quotient rule and the chain rule.

**Step 1:** Apply the quotient rule to differentiate F(x)

The quotient rule states that if F(x) = G(x)/H(x), then F'(x) = (G'(x)H(x) - G(x)H'(x)) / H(x)^2

In this case, G(x) = e^x and H(x) = 1 + e^x

So, we have:

F'(x) = ((e^x)'(1 + e^x) - e^x((1 + e^x)') ) / (1 + e^x)^2

**Step 2:** Differentiate G(x) and H(x)

Using the chain rule, we get:

G'(x) = (e^x)'
= e^x

H'(x) = (1 + e^x)'
= e^x

**Step 3:** Substitute the derivatives into the quotient rule formula

Now that we have G'(x) and H'(x), we can substitute them back into the quotient rule formula:

F'(x) = (e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
= (e^x + e^(2x) - e^(2x)) / (1 + e^x)^2
= (e^x) / (1 + e^x)^2

Simplifying, we get:

F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}

So, the derivative F'(x) is:

**Final Answer:**
{
  "Final Answer": "\frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}"
}

***********************I'm happy to help you with that problem. Here's my step-by-step solution:

The given function is y = 1/(x^2)

To find the derivative, we can use the power rule of differentiation, which states that if y = x^n, then y' = nx^(n-1).

In this case, n = -1 (since 1/x^2 can be written as x^(-2)).

So, using the power rule, we get:

dy/dx = d(1/x^2)/dx
= -2/x^3

Therefore, the derivative of y = 1/(x^2) is dy/dx = -2/x^3.

***********************৩য় শারীরবৃত্তের উপপাদ্য ব্যবহার করে, ln x এর চলন্দরাণুর ডেরিভেটিভ 1/x, তাই:

F(x) = xln x

Derivative of F(x) = d/dx (xln x)
= x * d/dx (ln x) + ln x * d/dx (x)

Using the power rule and chain rule of differentiation:
= x * (1/x) + ln x * 1
= 1 + ln x

So, the derivative of F(x) = xln x is:

{'Final Answer': '1+ln(x)'}

Which is the same result we got earlier!

F (x) = e^(x^2) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ খুঁজে পেতে, আমাদের প্রথমে ফাংশনটির একটি চলকের সাপেক্ষে ডেরিভেটিভ খুঁজে বের করতে হবে।

আমরা উপযোগী নিয়ম এবং চলকের সাপেক্ষে ডেরিভেটিভ দ্বারা সহজ আছে

F(x) = e^(x^2)

F'(x) = 2xe^(x^2)

এখন, আমরা F'(x) ফাংশনটিকে পুনরাবৃত্তি করে ডেরিভেটিভ নিই।

F''(x) = D(F'(x))

D(xe^(x^2)) = (1)(xe^(x^2))+ x(2e^(x^2)) + e^(x^2)(2x)

= xe^(x^2) + 2xe^(x^2) + 2xe^(x^2)

F''(x) = 5xe^(x^2)

সুতরাং, F(x) = e^(x^2) এর দ্বিতীয় ডেরাইভেটিভ হল:

{'Final Answer' : '5xe^(x^2)'}

***********************I'd be happy to help you find the derivative of F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2}.

To find the derivative, we can use the quotient rule. The quotient rule states that if f(x) and g(x) are functions, then the derivative of their quotient is given by:

\frac{d}{dx} \left( \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \right) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{(g(x))^2}

In this case, we have:

F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2}

We can identify f(x) = x and g(x) = 1 + x^2.

Now, let's find the derivatives of f(x) and g(x):

f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x) = 1

g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (1 + x^2) = 2x

Now we can plug these values into the quotient rule formula:

F'(x) = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}

Simplifying, we get:

F'(x) = \frac{1 + x^2 - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}

F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}

So, the derivative of F(x) is \boxed{\frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}}.

{
  "Final Answer" : "\frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}"
}

***********************A nice calculus question!

Let's break it down step by step:

The given function is: `∫(x^2) dx`

We need to find the derivative of this integral, which means we'll apply the fundamental theorem of calculus.

First, let's evaluate the integral:
`∫(x^2) dx = (1/3)x^3 + C`

Now, we take the derivative of this result with respect to x:
`(d/dx)((1/3)x^3 + C) = (d/dx)(1/3)x^3 + 0 (since dC/dx = 0)`

Using the power rule for differentiation, which states that `d(x^n)/dx = nx^(n-1)` for any n ≠ -1:
`(d/dx)((1/3)x^3) = (1/3)(3)x^(3-1) = x^2`

So, the derivative of the given integral is:

`{ "Final Answer": "x^2" }`
